93. SYDNEY JOHNSON (49) , Unlawfully obtaining 100 l. and other sums from Joseph Everett and others, with intent to defraud. MESSRS. MONTAGU WILLIAMS and MEAD Prosecuted; MR. FILLAN Defended.
JOHN WOOD . I am a beer retailer at the Ale Stores, 2, Park Terrace, Markfield Road, South Tottenham—on Friday, 18th February, 1881, I saw this advertisement in the Daily Chronicle —I made a copy of if.( This teas for a manager for a first-class of licensed beer house; 75 l. being required as a deposit. Apply at 2, Gunton Placet Peckham Rye.) I saw another advertisement in the Daily Chronicle on Monday, 7th February, 1881—I madethiscopy of it at the same time.( This was far a man and wife to manage a first-class off licensed house in the West End; 150 l. required as security. Apply at the same address.) I went to 2, Gunton Place on the 18th—I saw Mr. Larking, whom I afterwards found to be the prisoner's son-in-law—from what he said I went to the Surrey Arms beer-house, near the Crystal Palace Road—I looked at the house and then went to Gunton Place and saw Larking, who introduced me to the prisoner—I said "The Surrey Arms was not good, enough for me"—he said "I have other houses; there is one at Sydenham, that possibly would suit you "—he gave me an address—I afterwards went to No. 2, Park Terrace, Markfield Road, South Tottenham—a Mr. Kellod was in possession, and the name up was "Henry Philip Larking, licensed to sell beer to be consumed off the premises "—the next day I went back to see the prisoner—I said the house was not so good as I expected, but I would take it and do the best I could for him with it—I said "Are the rent and taxes paid and is there a lien on the house?"—he answered "No, I have bought it as a present for my son-in-law "—he asked me if I was prepared with 75 l.—I said I had got the money at home, but seeing the size of the house and the business I thought 40 l. would be sufficient—I agreed to pay it—I paid him the 40 l. deposit—I told him I held an appointment for one of the City Companies, but with the exception of eight to twelve days a quarter my time was my own—he said he was a Freemason and a Vintner—I engaged to meet him the following Monday—when I met him on Monday, 21st February, he produced this agreement—I paid him 20 l. deposit—I went into possession the following Saturday, 26th, when I paid him the remaining 20 l., and the agreement was signed and the matter settled—my wife said to the prisoner" My husband is being defrauded out of his money "—Johnson said "Nothing of the kind, the money is as safe as it is in the bank; you can get it back at any time"—he said more stock was coming in the following Monday, and that the house was to be thoroughly decorated and cleaned, and the gas laid on—he said the licence was in the name of Larking—nothing was done to the house—a barrel of ale was sent once or twice in the two months—I went and told Larking I had no beer—when the prisoner ceased to supply me I bought beer for myself—in consequence of a conversation between me and Larking on 30th April, I saw Johnson and he handed me this notice.( To deliver up the premises that day month.) Larking and Shine were present—Johnson said he was on the point of disposing of
the business to Mr. Hicks, and Mr. Shine was to make an application with regard to the licence—he said he would settle with me—on 25th May I received this letter from him.( To arrange to stop in the house so that it should not be closed before the 14 th June, and promising to settle if no arrangement was made with the brewers.) He did not keep his promise to settle although I made no arrangement with the brewers—an action of ejectment was brought against me, in consequence of which I delivered up the premises about the middle of December—I afterwards took possession as tenant of Mr. Parks, the freeholder—I paid 15 s. property tax and got this receipt; also for duties and licences 4 l., 16 s.4 3/4 d —I was still a tenant of the freeholder—I have not received my deposit back from the prisoner—I have written several times for it—I have received no answer—one letter was returned through the Dead Letter Office—I have tried to see Johnson, but could never find him—an exciseman called about my selling beer, in consequence of which I paid him excess money.
Cross-examined. I had not been a publican before—I did not consult my wife—I do sometimes—she is a sensible business woman—I took the beerhouse on my unaided judgment—my wife was against my going there—I have returned a statement of account to the prisoner—he has not sent me beer to the amount of 60 l., but only 13 l.11 s. 6 d.; there were twenty one gallons of ale there when I went, but it was so bad that people would not drink it—I did not try it before I took the house—I contracted to buy the beer from him. and I did so up to a certain date—he did not send me in an account—he was to allow me 13 s. a barrel—this is the book I kept—the first date is 26th February—he did not send me two barrels and a half a week for nine weeks—I have accounted to him for the whole of the money I spent, including wages and expenses—I was his servant—I asked him permission to see to the drains, and he said he was going to do it—I have nine children—the soil flooded the yard—the account for the drains is entered in the book, 1 l. 15 s.—that would go into the decoration of the house if he had done it—I took him to be a gentleman—I did not decorate the house—I did what I considered essential to health—you will see he is only charged 2 s. a week—the first week I was there I took 1 l. 7 s. 4 d.—I do not sell a barrel a week—I have no hours at my place in the City; I go when I am sent for, about eight or twelve times a quarter—I sent my statement to the Treasury after I saw the case in the Weekly Dispatch —I tried to get legal aid, but could not get it—I would have liked to have bothered Johnson if I could have got at him—I went to his beerhouses, but they would not tell me where he was—Mr. Shines did not tell me that Worthington and Co. had to pay him 850 l. under an arbitration—I believe Johnson is known all over Peckham—I found his character was respectable and honest till I was taken in—I did not go to Gordon and Co.—I went to Whitbread's and inquired, and Johnson said it was a nice thing to do, and so I let it stand in abeyance, and that threw me back in my inquiries for some time.
Re-examined. The house was in a bad state of repair and very dirty when I went in—the prisoner said he would thoroughly repair and clean the place—he did not do that—I applied to him to do it and to do the drains—he put me off—he owes me altogether 78 l.—my only reason for taking proceedings was because I could not find him.
By MR. FILLAN. The fixtures were not valued at 36 l.—I cannot value them—I have not paid for them—I have got them still—there is a beer engine—I do not know its value—I thought the fixtures were the landlord's—I entered the house as a servant.
By MR. MEAD. I do not claim the beer-engine nor fixtures.
JOHN RAE . I am a clerk to Mr. E.W. Parks, Mansion House Chambers, solicitor—he is the landlord of 2, Park Terrace, Tottenham—this is his signature to this lease for 28 years from 29th September, 1879, of the premises in question to Sydney Johnson—it is dated 7th October—the rent reserved was 40 l. per annum.—no rent has been paid to Mr. Parks—I produce writ of ejectment of 25th November in an action by Mr. Parks—I also produce the judgment giving possession to Mr. Parks—possession was taken under that writ, and Wood was turned out—he was reinstated as Mr. Parka's tenant a few days after the judgment—I should say it was 17th December that I recovered possession from the Sheriff, and handed it over to Wood—Mr. Parks would claim the fixtures.
Cross-examined. The house was built about six or seven years ago—I have no entry in my books of rent being paid—we have two of Johnson's actions pending—I have not produced the proceedings, and had no notice—I believe the actions have now dropped—I did not know they were against Worthington and Co. for 1,000 l. and 1,300 l.—the rent being in arrear 21 days, on our bringing an action of ejectment the lease is forfeited.
ARNOLD AUSTEN . I am clerk to Messrs. Linklater, of Walbrook, solicitors to the trustee in Worthington and Co's. liquidation—Worthington's were brewers—amongst other documents I received this agreement between Johnson and Worthington and Co., of 25th November, 1877 ( A lease of 2, Park; Terrace assigned to Worthington and Co. in consideration of 150 l., subject to a penalty of 51. on Johnson's procuring beer elsewhere)—I also received this agreement of 1st October ( between Arthur Fell Worthington, William Charles Marshall, and Sydney Johnson, to assign Johnson's interest in five premises, scheduled to Worthington's for 872 l. 11 s. 3 d.)—the debt has not been recovered—no award has been made by any arbitrator under the agreement.
Cross-examined. The schedule includes the house Mr. Wood had—there is a provision for an arbitrator—I am not aware that an arbitrator has been appointed, and has valued the house at 135 l. odd—I believe two arbitrators were appointed, but they disagreed—I do not know the value of the premises assigned to Worthington's.
WILLIAM LAMB SHINE . I was manager to Messrs. Worthington—the prisoner was their general agent—850 l. was secured from the prisoner to Worthington's by the agreement of October, 1880—I promised him compensation, which he never got—about 100 l. should come off—then Johnson supplied fixtures to different houses—I left Worthington's before their liquidation.
Cross-examined. The prisoner expended 350 l. in fittings against the debt of 1,142 l. upon the different houses—we sold bad beer, ruined our business, and went into bankruptcy—the five houses were valued by the prisoner's valuer at 1,350 l.—the arbitrators had to decide the account between Worthington and Johnson—I heard at Lambeth Police-court that one of the five houses had sold for 750 l.
Re-examined. Worthington's valuer valued the five houses at 750 l.
EDWARD POWELL . I am an engineer, of 1, Larcher Terrace, Victoria Docks—before June, 1881, I had saved some money, and had 100 l. to invest—about that time J saw an advertisement in Lloyd's Newspaper for a manager of an off licensed house—I went to the address indicated, and saw the prisoner—I said I had come about the advertisement—he said he had three or four houses, and he took me and showed me one at 33, Winster Terrace, Maxted Road, Feckham—it was then occupied—I saw the bar and the adjoining room—the name up was "Sydney Johnson, licensed to sell beer, not to be drunk on the premises"—there was not much fitting a deal counter and a rail for the casks, no beer-engine—I said, "Who does the house belong to? who is the landlord?"—he said, "It is my own, and if 1 was going to sell it I should want 500 l. for it," and "I have three or four more you can see, and have the choice of which you like "—he pointed to a plot of ground at the corner of the street and said, "That is mine, on which I intend to build a tavern; when I have done that I shall shut this up, take the licence from it, and put it to the new"—I made an arrangement and met him again two or three days after at Cyprus Terrace—I paid him 5 l. deposit; this is his receipt—when I next met him I paid him 45 l.—this is his receipt of June 18,1881—I paid him the remaining 50 l. when I took possession on 22nd June—some beer was in the house—I made him two payments for beer as he got it in, the full retail prices; first 17 l. for what was called beer, but afterwards turned out a good deal of it waste—application had then been made for rent; I told him so—he said he did not know what he had to pay it with, and he paid it out of the 17 l.—the rent demanded was 8 l. 6 s. 8 d. for two months—he said they were going to put the brokers in—the next occasion I paid him 14 l., and 4 l. was due to me for wages, but I allowed that to go on towards paying, making it 18 l.—I discovered it was a monthly tenancy when the demand for rent came—I afterwards paid 3 l. 1 s. 4 d. a month—on 13th July I sent the prisoner through my solicitor notice to leave his service—on August 16th he came and handed me this notice ( To quit that day month, and promising to settle according to agreement, holding the witness responsible for any damage or for letters returned through the Dead Letter Office)—I did not know where he could be found, and after keeping the letters a time I handed them to the lettercarrier—once after the payment of the 18 l. he came and said, "Well, Powell, what cash have you for me?"—I said, "None, Mr. Johnson "—he said, "How is that?"—I said,"I am advised not to pay you any more"—I owed him more than 20 l. for beer, I cannot say how much; here is the book—he paid me four weeks' wages—I had been in his service four weeks. up to the notice—he has not repaid me any part of the 100 l.—I did not know where he lived—I have since paid the rent.
Cross-examined. I could not say I was in his service after my refusal to pay him—the landlord's agent came for the rent—Johnson's son lived nearly opposite me—I had no occasion to go to him, and I was expecting Johnson to come and settle with me—I sold the goodwill of the house for 30 l., not 75 l., to the brewer that supplied me, because I owed him some money.
Re-examined. I was in the house from June till March—I was the landlord's tenant from November—I had not seen the prisoner since August—the licence was transferred on 14th December to the brewers—the takings were about 15 l.a week in the summer, and in the winter season very bad. A——J—— MORRIS, I am agent for Mr. Bailey, of 13, Winster
Terrace, the landlord of this beerhouse—I let it on Mr. Bailey's behalf to Mr. Powell—Worthington's the brewers were my previous tenants—I let it for 12 months, and then from month to month—Johnson paid the rent till Powell came after the tenancy was monthly—the receipts were made out to Worthington's—Johnson said the licence was granted in his name, and he should like the receipts in his name—I said, "I cannot give you a receipt in your own name as we consider Worthington's are tenants"—the rent was payable on the first Monday in each month—I accepted Mr. Powell as tenant when Worthington's were served with notice to quit.
Cross-examined. The prisoner was never my tenant—Worthington's had no power to underlet—I could not swear there was a written agreement because I did not let it—I have searched for a counterpart and have not found it.
HENRY EVANS . I am a licensed victualler, but live privately—in August, 1881, I saw this advertisement in Lloyd's Newspaper ( For a manager for on off beerhouse, deposit 50 l.)—I went to the address stated—I saw the prisoner in the Aldcrshot Road—I said, "I am come respecting the offlicence that was advertised in Lloyd's "—he said the house was at 99, High Street, Sydenham—I had a conversation with him about it, and told him I would go and see it—I saw and approved of it—I saw the prisoner several days after; I told him I should take the house, but should not be able to pay 50 l., I should not be able to find more than 40 l.—he said be had been losing a lot of money by the house, and he must have some one. in it to open it on the Saturday following, the 15th August—the house was closed at the time—I agreed to pay him 40 l.—I asked him if the house was all right—he told me "Yes," and I could open and sell the next morning if I liked—I paid him 5 l. deposit—I went into possession on 15th August—the agreement and receipt for 40 l. which I paid him is dated 15th August—he said he would send beer in so that it could open on the Saturday—I did not open on the Saturday—no beer was sent in the whole time I was there till I was ejected—no water was on, that was supplied three weeks after—the excise officer called in September and made a statement, that was Mr. Bailey—I never sold because there was no beer and no licence—possession was taken of the premises by the Sheriff the latter end of September, and I had to go out—I was readmitted as care-taker by the landlord's agent—I applied for my money back, both by letter and verbally—he said he had given me a month's notice according to the agreement, and when it was up he would pay me—he never served mo with a month's notice, I served one on him—I did not get my money back—I received this letter from him ( September 15 l A, 1881, stating that he [ Johnson ] was going to see about the licence)—I applied for a licence and obtained it on 10th January—the prisoner appeared and told the Magistrate he had been robbed out of his property—he paid me back 5 l., and then 15 l. the end of 1881—1 brought an action in the High Court against him—the 5 l. I received was "for wages due "—he requested me to add those words—my solicitor received 35 s
Cross-examined. I swore in my affidavit in the action brought by my solicitor that the defendant was indebted to me 25 l., the balance of 45 l., deposited with me—I did not take out a commitment summons—he did not pay me 1 l., the first and 1 l. the second mouth—a judgment summons was taken out, and he appeared to it in March—I sold the house to Mr. Williamson, for 60 l.—I had to pay that away—I got nothing to go out-;
the tenant got from Mr. Morgan 60 l.—Mr. Morgan's tenant became tenant after getting a licence—I got notice to leave from Mr. Morgan, of Forest Hill—I was merely tenant for Mr. Morgan; that was this year.
ALBERT YOUNG .I am clerk to Messrs. Drake and Co., solicitors, of Rood Lane, City, solicitors to Mr. Hughes, the owner of these premises, 99, High Street, Sydenham—I produce counterpart of lease signed by the prisoner, of 25th November, 1879, for 21 years from Mr. Hughes, or at the option of the tenant seven or 14 years, at 45 l. a year net premium—I had to issue a writ of ejectment for non-payment of rent—I signed judgment, put in an execution, and took possession—I gave possession to Mr. May, and May let Evans stop in—he was Mr. Hughes's auctioneer and house agent—the rent claimed was 11 l. 5 s. due 24th June, 1881.
Cross-examined. I cannot tell what the lease would be worth; no premium has been paid for it.
CHARLES BRAYLEY . I am an excise officer for the Forest Hill district, in Kent—99, High Street, Sydenham, is in the district—the licence expired in July, 1881—I called upon Evans in consequence of the premises not being licensed—they were licensed again in January, 1882—the entry is on the 12th; the licence was taken up a few days before that—the duties due in July would be about 5 l. 5 s., 3 l.6 s. for the wholesale,1 l. 5 s. for the retail, and then there would be the tobacco—those duties must be paid before beer is sold.
WILLIAM LIMB SHINE ( Re-examined).I witnessed this agreement of January 30th, 1880.( The assignment of the prisoner's interest to Wor thington's for 151)
HENRY EVANS ( Re-examined).I was sent for to the Treasury; I had not been to the police-court before that.
JOSEPH EVERETT . I live at 228, Crystal Palace Road, East Dulwich, formerly known as 30, Rossmore Road—I was a foreman platelayer, and had saved 100 l., which I was anxious to invest—I saw an advertisement in Lloyds Newspaper. ( To the effect that a man and wife were required to manage an off licence, and that application was to be made to 1, Neville Road, South Hackney.) I wrote an answer to that, and received this reply. ( This was dated Lordship Terrace, Lordship Lane, 4th May, 1882, and signed by the prisoner, stating he required a manager for an off licence at Lordship Lane; that the witness had said nothing as to cash security, and that he had teveral houses.) I wrote to the address to the effect that I could find cash security, and received this letter from the prisoner, dated 6th May.( Asking him to call en the prisoner, and saying that he had several public-houses.) I called that evening, but did not see him—I saw him on the 18th—I called at Lordship Terrace, but was directed to 208, Crystal Palace Road, where I am now living—I said to him "I have come in answer to the advertisement I saw in the paper as to a manager"—he said "Yes, this is the house "—we went in and looked over the house—I noticed alterations being carried out; a counter was being fitted up and a wooden shed built outside in the back yard—I did not see a beerengine at that time or any excavation for a cellar—he said he proposed opening the house as an off-licence beerhouse—I said "I should like that house "—he said he thought of coming in that one himself, but he finally said I might have it—I said "Do you owe any money on the house?"—be said "No, not a farthing"—I said "Have you a licence for selling beer at 208, Crystal Palace Road?"—he said "Yes, already
filled up; it only wants your name put on it"—he did not say where the licence was then, but on the 11th, before I paid any money to him, he said he had it in his own possession—I called at 9, Lordship Terrace, and looked at it, and ultimately decided I would take this one in the Crystal Palace Road—I took my wife to see it on the 11th—in her presence he said he had the licence in his own possession, and it was already filled up, and only wanted my name put on it if I had the cash security—he said that two or three times—I said "On the faith of that I will pay 50 l. as a deposit"—he agreed to accept that—after we got into the house he said if I had that house he should require me to pay 100 l.—I then paid 50 l. on account, and he gave me this receipt:"9, Lordship Terrace, May 11, 1882. Received of Mr. Everett the sum of 50 l. in part payment of 100 l. security on taking the ale and wine stores, 208, Crystal Palace Road. The balance to be paid on such date as arranged in Mr. Johnson's agreement. "On 17th May I took possession, and I then paid the remaining 50 l.—this is the agreement—I was to be manager at 1 l. a week; I was allowed to sell mineral waters and any extras I liked, and to have the profit on that, and I was to occupy the house and pay no rent; he would pay the rent, rates, and taxes—I was to do nothing to jeopardise the licence—I was not to take lodgers without his sanction—the alterations were not completed when I went in—a cellar was being excavated for the beer; they did not carry it further after I went in, and it is not finished now—beer was sent in by the prisoner on the Thursday, the day after I took possession—there were no stores for a grocer's business, or anything of that sort—on 27th May I opened the premises for the sale of beer—a beer engine had been fitted like an ordinary beerhouse—I asked him for a licence before I opened—he told me several times I need not fear; it was all right with the police, and no one would interfere with me—on 30th May George John Sayers, agent to Mr. Baxter, the brewer, called on me and made a communication, and on 7th June I saw the prisoner and spoke to him in reference to Mr. Sayers's visit—I said that I had been told that Mr. Baxter held the licence for the security of the stock and fixtures, and I asked him for the licence—he said he would go and get it at once—I did not go with him to Mr. Baxter's; he made all sorts of excuses, and said "I have somewhere else to go"—I went alone to Mr. Baxter's at the stores—I was told something there, and while there the prisoner came—I said "I have been given to understand that the licence is at 12, Furnival's Inn, at the solicitor's there"—I went to the solicitor's with Johnson for the purpose of getting the licence—the solicitor would not give it up, as he said it was there as security for money owed by Johnson—on 21st June I saw Johnson and told him that a gentleman had been for the purpose of serving him with a writ to restrain me from selling beer, wine, or spirits either on or off the premises—Johnson said "That is nothing to you; you are my servant; I will fight the battle with him"—I said "I will close the shop"—he said "No"—on the next day he called on me and suggested that I should keep out of the way and not receive this writ—the next day I was served with the writ.( This was a writ of summons from the British Empire Mutual Insurance Company, Limited, against Thomas Twiddy, Sydney Johnson, and Joseph Everett, dated 20th June, to restrain the defendants from carrying on or permitting to be carried on at 228, Lordship Terrace the sale of beer, wines, or spirits without the consent of the plaintiff in writing, and from making any cellar or alteration on the pre
mises.) I showed that to my solicitor, and, acting on his advice, closed the shop—some beer had been sent in, and I had been selling it—afterwards judgment was served on me to restrain me from selling—Johnson had sent in 29 l. worth of beer—I had sold and delivered altogether, draught and bottled, about 12 l.2 s. worth, and altogether I had taken in the shop 13 l. 3 s. 4 d.—I did not pay any money to Johnson for that beer—I did not receive any money for salary—after the injunction was served I asked Johnson for my money back several times—he said he had not the money to pay me with while he was at law—I last saw him before he was charged at Lambeth Police-court on 10th July in Lordship Lane, close to his house—I asked him then for my money—I have never been paid one farthing of my 100 l.—I went with Mr. Bennett and Mr. Hatton and made a complaint to Mr. Ellison, the Magistrate, at the police-court, and after that communicated with the Solicitor to the Treasury, and under their direction laid an information, on which the warrant was issued.
Cross-examined. I first looked over 9, Lordship Lane, I never intended to take it, and then over 228, Crystal Palace Road—he wanted 75 l. for the Lordship Lane one and 100 l. for the Crystal Palace Road—I chose the latter after looking over both—there was a shed erected in the back yard, and a cellar was being excavated and a beer engine, counter, and fittings being erected when I looked in on the 17th—I signed the agreement and went in—I knew I was only allowed to sell beer off the premises—I have heard that the British Empire Company had given a lease of the house seven years before to Mr. Bendall, but not to sell beer—I only sold beer to be drunk on the premises to Mr. Johnson and his sons; they led me to do it—two clerks from the British Empire Company were not served with two glasses of ale; I will swear it—Mr. Johnson was served with the injunction—I know the British Empire Company allowed beer to be served off but not on the premises—I cannot say if the British Empire Company allowed Bendall to serve beer off the premises for seven years, but forbade Bendall or Twiddy serving it on the premises—I was not allowed to sell it on the premises—I did it to Mr. Johnson and his son; he said he had no doubt some of his pals would call on the Sunday morning for wets —1 did not agree to that—I think the British Empire Company served me with the injunction because they would not allow beer to be sold there—I know nothing about Bendall—I have sold nothing in the house since I shut it up; I am living there—the beer engine and counter are there, and what is left of the beer—Johnson did not send in more than 50 l. worth of beer—when I said at the police-court I did not know the value of the stock on the premises I had not been over it then—I have used some myself since—my pals did not come for a wet —I am a stranger in the place—it is only the injunction prevents me selling—when I asked Johnson to pay me all he said was he was at law—he did not tell me he was at law with Messrs. Worthington with regard to a claim he had given them—he was too artful to tell me anything but that—it would not pay me to sell groceries or mineral waters there, nor to open it as a general shop—the prisoner did not tell me he would sell off the beer and fittings and pay me out of that; he told me to go to the brewers' agent and see what they would allow me; he did not mention the fittings—he sent word on 7th August by Almond to know what I wanted of him I never went to the British Empire Company to ask for
their consent to carry on any business—I would not consent to the brewers taking the beer, and I would not wait till he had finished with Worth-ington's—he did not say I was to have the value of the beer, and he did not ask me to wait—he told me he was at law and would pay me—he can have the beer when my money is forthcoming—I should never have got my money if I had gone to a civil court; I am thinking of the public—100 l. would not repay me now—I have paid 25 l. for the action—I knew the licence was ready at the solicitors, only wanting my name—the bar and fixtures are mortgaged.
Re-examined. I have no more money to buy grocery stock—Mr. Twiddy allows me to stop in the house—I know that the defendant is a bankrupt; he served me with a notice—I have here all the bills referring to beer which has been supplied to me, and they come to 29 l., including everything—if he paid me my 100 l. and my expenses I should be willing to give the beer up—the action with the British Empire Company cost me 25 l., and will cost me 5 l. more, besides all the loss of time—the shop is now fitted as a beershop—I have no interest in the fixtures, and could make no claim on them; they belong to the landlord—my wife is here.
ARTHUR EDWARD TWIDDY . I live at 211, High Street, Borough, and am a wholesale provision merchant—John Waite Bendall was formerly a customer of mine—on 10th June, 1881, I was managing the business; he owed my father money, and in liquidation assigned him the lease of the house in question in Crystal Palace Road, formerly called the' Boss-more Road—the assignment is endorsed on an agreement for a lease between the British Empire Company and John Waite Bendall for 99 years, I think, from year to year for 15 years, and then at the expiration of that time the tenant may nave the option of a lease for 84 years. ( One of the clauses of the agreement was read, stating that Bendall teas not to carry on any trade, business, or manufacture without the consent of the company in writing.) Mr. Bendall carried on the business of a grocer and cheesemonger on those premises, and used to sell bottled beer to be consumed off the premises—I had supplied him with the groceries—he left the premises; I cannot say when—I endeavoured to let the premises before he went—I advertised them for sale, and spoke to Johnson about them—Johnson called on me about the end of March last year, and said he had called about the shop in the Crystal Palace Road—he said he wanted to make it a central depot for bottling beer, as he had a number of off-licence houses, and would supply them from there—I told him he could not open it as a beershop, as we could have let it to Mr. Miller only the British Empire would not strike the clause out of the original lease—I said he could carry on a bottled-beer trade there the same as Bendall, and in con-junction with a grocer's business—he did not say he wanted it for a beershop—nothing was said with regard to draught beer—I showed him the agreement with the superior landlord—he read it, and said it was quite satisfactory—he read it right through—I don't know that I pointed out that particular clause—I did not give my consent to a beerengine being put on the premises; I did not know there was one, nor that a cellar was being excavated—we made all the necessary repairs that Johnson required before he went in. ( The agreement between Twiddy and Johnson, was dated April 8, 1882, set forth a yearly tenancy at a rent of 40 l. a year, and contained a clause that the tenant should be allowed to carry on a grocer's trade with the sale of trims and beer under an existing licence after application
to the Justices) Johnson said to me "It is the last day for renewing the licence, and it the licence is lost the property will drop 100 l.," and we gave him the money to go and take the licence out—Mr. Bendull had the licence—I was served with a copy of a writ restraining me from the sale—the first quarter's rent was paid in advance—when the next quarter became due, in September, we put in an execution—the agreement does not say the rent is to be paid in advance, but he did so—we took possession of the premises, but allow Mr. Everett to remain there—I believe we distrained for the rent; we regard the fixtures as ours.
Cross-examined. The rent was paid by Johnson up to 21st June, and no rent was due till 29th September—he would have 21 days after the 29th to pay it—I do not know he was arrested on the 29th—I do not think he has lost the 21 years' lease by it; it was from year to year—I do not think the 21 years is at his option; he was going to buy the place of us—I negotiated for it to be a shop where they sold beer off and on; that the British Empire Company would not agree to—they allowed beer and wines to be sold off on sufferance, as Bendall did; there was no agreement—I made an agreement with the tenant that he should sell wines and spirits off, but he knew all about it—the British Empire would not have put in an injunction if he had done it as it was before—he never said he was going to sell beer; he said he was going to make it a depot—I contend lie could not put up the beer-engine and sell wines and spirits by this agreement or make it a beerhouse.
Re-examined. The British Empire Company had not given me permission to put up a beer-engine or excavate—we have nearly lost our interest in it—they did not consent to draught beer being sold there—I did not explain to Johnson the difference between the two terms; I told him what Bendall sold—it was explained to Johnson he could carry on a grocer's business with the sale of bottled beer.
GEORGE JOHN SAYER . I am manager to Mr. Baxter, the brewer—about February, 1882: the prisoner dealt with us for beer, and we supplied it to several houses for him—in April he owed us 120 l.—we advanced him 150 l., giving him a cheque for 100 l. and setting off 50 l. against what he owed—in consequence of the indebtedness he entered into this agreement with us. ( This stated that Sydney Johnson deposited with Richard Baxter the deed mentioned in the schedule relating to the house as security for the 150 l. advanced, promised to deal with Baxter for all beer, paying for the name periodically, and undertook to mortgage the house to him for any sums further due, such mortgage to contain a power of sale.) I advanced him more money and supplied him with more beer—he owes me 270 l. now—this agreement of 8th April, 1882, is for letting 228, Crystal Palace Road—there are two licences for that and three for 2, Wakely Road, East Dulwich—those licences were in Mr. Bendall's name; they have never been transferred; I still hold them as collateral security.
Cross-examined. I have not got other licences in blank of these premises—here could not be two licences of one premises—this licence is in force till 5th April, 1883—it is endorsed by Bendall—it cannot be used by anybody else till it has been transferred by the Magistrate—it would be available for Mr. Everett after it had been transferred to him—the signature of J. W. Bendall on the licence means that he has no interest in it after that—it does not show to whom he assigns it—when Johnson
deposited the agreement with us he left the licence also—the licence is only a collateral security, the real thing is the agreement—only we should see that the licence was transferred to a respectable man—we wanted Everett to have it—brewers often take a regular mortgage for debt—there was nothing irregular or wrong in his depositing this with you, it was an ordinary thing; we should want some security when we advanced the money—if we had not we should not have given the beer—he never executed a mortgage.
Re-examined. When I called on Everett after he opened I learnt he had advanced 100 l. to Johnson—I spoke to Johnson about the transfer of the licence to Everett—I said he must have the licence transferred to some one, and "as Everett has told me he advanced 100 l. I have made inquiries; he is a respectable man, and the proper man to have it trans-ferred to "—Johnson said "I do not think he is fit for it, I shall go there myself; I shall have it in my own name"—he made an application to me for it—I said if he brought Everett up I would transfer it to Everett, but it was not to go out of our possession till it was transferred to Everett.
CHARLESYINEY ( Detective Sergeant). On 30th September I received a warrant for the prisoner's apprehension, and apprehended him on that day in Leadenhall Street—I read it to him and charged him with obtain-ing from Everett 100 l., and 40 l. from Evans—he said "I did not think they could do that"—I said "They have done it"—he said "Everett has more than enough in the house to pay him, and Evans has had 151. out of the house and another 5 l. "—he said afterwards "Everybody would have had their money, but Worthington's people owe me 2,000 l.; they would all have been paid "—I searched him at the station and found 251. in gold, 5a. in silver, and a watch and chain.
A. J. MORRIS ( Re-examined). No. 13, Winchester Terrace, belonged to Mr. Bailey, and the plot at the other corner belonged to him too—Mr. Johnson has no interest in that—there is a footpath across the plot.
Cross-examined. I am Mr. Bailey's agent—'Mr. Bailey told me he had assigned the plot to Worthington's—I can swear he had told Worthing-ton the plot cannot be dealt with, I heard it repeatedly—I can't remember when, some time ago—since then I cannot tell what representations he has made.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY ,— Ten Months' Hard labour.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

95. JAMES WALSH (23) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin. MESSRS. POLAND and CHARLES MATHEWS Prosecuted.
ANNIE HOLLAND . I am a barmaid at the Sun in Union Street, kept by Mr. Butcher—about half-past 3 o'clock on 14th October the prisoner came in and asked for half a pint of ale, that would be 1 1/2 d.—he tendered in payment half a crown—I gave him 2 sis. 4 1/2d. change, and handed the
half-crown to Mr. Butcher—my master went out and returned, and the prisoner was still there—when I left the bar at about 4 o'clock the prisoner was still there.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I did not know at the time you were standing at the bar that it was a bad half-crown.
AUGUSTUS ADOLPHUS BUTCHER . I keep the Sun public-house—on 14th October, about 3 o'clock, the last witness gave me a half-crown—I put it in my pocket, where there was no other half-crown, but only a shilling, two sixpences, a penny, and two fourpennies—I went out to order some cans, and on my way was stopped by some customers—I took them into the Fountain and Grapes to give them some beer, and I took out the half-crown, and the manager, Mr. Norman, threw it down and broke it—I went on and ordered my cans, and then went back—the prisoner was not there then, he came back subsequently and asked me to have a glass of wine with him—I did, and then my barmaid came in and made a communication to me—I said nothing to him—I afterwards went out with Clarke after him, and found him in custody at the station.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. The barmaid was not there when I had a glass of wine with you—my wife was there.
Re-examined. I gave the half-crown returned to me by Norman at the Fountain and Grapes to the constable at the station, where it was broken.
HENRY NORMAN . I manage the Fountain and Grapes—on Saturday, 14th October, Mr. Butcher brought me this half-crown, which I tested and handed back to Mr. Butcher.
LOUISA WALL . I am barmaid at the Brown Bear, Southwark Bridge Road—on 14th October, about 4.30p.m., the prisoner came in and asked for a small lemonade, which came to 2 d.—I served him—he gave me this coin, and I gave him change—I gave the coin to my sister—the prisoner left then, but returned subsequently between 6 and 7 o'clock, and whilst standing in the bar the constable came and arrested him.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I did not hear what the barman said to you when the policeman was there, but I know you are the man who changed the first half-crown.
MARY ANN WALL . This half-crown was given me by my sister—I saw it was a bad one—I put it in my pocket, and afterwards handed it to a police constable.
WILLIAM CLARKE . I am barman at the Henry VIII. in Union street—on 14th October the prisoner came in between 5 and 6 o'clock and called for half a pint of stout-and-mild, the price of which would be 1 1/2 d.—he tendered in payment this half-crown ( produced)—I gave him 2 s. 4 1/2 d. change—I kept the half-crown in my hand and went to the proprietor—the coin was bad, and I went after the prisoner—on my way I met Policeman N 234, to whom I spoke and gave the half-crown—we went together in search of the prisoner, and found him at the Brown Bear—I showed the prisoner the half-crown, and told the constable he was the man—the prisoner did not say anything—I took him back to the Henry VIII.—he afterwards gave me back the change I had given him—1 gave the half-crown to the constable.
Cross-examined. I do not know you as working for Mr. Newton—his men use my house—I have seen you in the house. JOHN ARTHUR MARKHAM ( Policeman JV 243). On 14th October I met
the last witness, and he gave me tins counterfeit half-crown—I wont to the Brown Bear and found the prisoner —I took him back to the Henry VIII., and the barman there said "That was a bad half-crown you gave me "—the prisoner paid "I did not know it was bad, but I am willing to give you half a crown in good money if you take no notice," and he gave him half a crown—I asked him first of all if he had any more bad money—I felt about him, but could find no more—the barman would not charge him, but I took him to the station and there searched him again—I found 3. in good silver and sevenpence in bronze—I noticed him throw something into his mouth—I caught hold of his throat and choked him for a moment, and he spat it out—I picked it up—it was this bad half-crown—I had kept the half-crown handed to me by Clarke in my hand—I made inquiries, and among other places went to the Brown Bear, and received there this bad half-crown from Louisa Wall.
Cross-examined. You did not put the half-crown in your teeth to try it at the station—I choked you for nearly two minutes before you would let it go out of your throat.
Re-examined. I went to the address the prisoner gave me at 5, St. Margaret's Court.
EDWARD HAYES . I am a waterman at the Metropolitan Tavern, South-wark Street—I saw the prisoner with the constable—I did not see the constable leave the prisoner—I was drawing water at the time.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner, I saw you standing close by the constable.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . I am assistant examiner of coins at the Queen's Mint—these four coins are all bad, and from two different moulds—the one found on the prisoner is from the same mould as one of the others.
Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "I had three half-crowns. I won them and a shilling at tossing. I did not know that they were bad. I changed one at the Sun. I never put my hand to my mouth, and I had every opportunity of throwing it away had I known it was bad. "
The prisoner, in his defence, repeated the statement.
GUILTY .
He then PLEADED GUILTY * to a conviction of felony in 1875 in the name of James Welsh .— Eighteen Months' Hard Labour.

96. GEORGE ALEXANDER (32) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND and CHARLES MATHEWS Prosecuted.
FLORENCE HOWE . I am assistant to Mr. John Howe, at Cleveland Road, North Cross Road, Dulwich—on 15th November the prisoner came, at 7.20. p.m., and asked for a bottle of Bass, which came to. 6 d.—he tendered a florin in payment, and I gave him 1 s.6 d. change and put the florin in the till—the prisoner left—soon after a woman came in, and while she was there a man came in and asked for a bottle of stout, which came to 6 d.—he gave me a two-shilling piece, and I gave him 1 s. 6 d. change, and placed it in the same till—I took both the florins to my father, and they were both bad—I went out and saw a constable, and gave him the bad coins and a description of the man who had tendered them—the same night I went to the police-station and saw the prisoner there—I charged him; he did not hear what I said.
JOHN BARRELL ( Policeman P 32). On 7th November, about a quarter
to 8, Miss Howe came and made a communication to me, and gave me a description—later in the evening, at half-past 8, I saw the prisoner in the Crystal Palace Road, about 10 minutes' walk from Mr. Howe's shop—he was in company with two other men—they all went to the Crystal Palace Tavern—I watched them come out and go down the Crystal Palace Road—one of the men gave the prisoner something, and he crossed over the road and went inside a shop—I slipped along to the end of the window, and looked inside, and saw the prisoner give the shopkeeper, Elizabeth Tuck, a two-shilling piece—Mrs. Tuck is a cripple, and unable to come here—she gave the prisoner the change, and he left—I went into the shop at once, spoke to Mrs. Tuck, and took a bad two-shilling piece from her hand in which she had taken it from the prisoner—I came out, and saw the prisoner cross the road—the other men were 15 or 20 yards in front—I went up to him, and said "Do you know you gave that woman a two-shilling piece?"—he said "What do you mean? I gave that woman a shilling—I saw at the time he tendered it that it was a florin—I took him into custody—the other two men slipped away—he was charged at the station, and made no answer to the charge—I searched him, and found half a crown, four shillings, a sixpence, and threepence in bronze, good money, and a pawn ticket—no bad money was found upon him—Mrs. Tuck's is a china and general shop—the prisoner bought a cup and saucer there—he threw it down and broke it in the North Cross Road—this is the coin; I bent it with my teeth—when I was looking through the window I was about six yards from the prisoner—I brushed by him as he was coming out.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These three florins are all bad, and from different moulds.
GUILTY .— Nine Months' hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18821120-97]
97. RICHARD RICHARDSON (51) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND and CHARLES MATHEWS Prosecuted.
WILLIAM STEVENS . I keep the Alliance public-house, at Peckham—on the afternoon of 22nd November the prisoner came in and called for half a pint of stout and mild—he put down a shilling—I tried it, and found it was bad—I said to the prisoner "This won't do, this is bad. You must give me my change back "—he said" I will pay you in half pence; it is very unfortunate. I had taken a two-shilling piece a fortnight ago that was bad "—I said "Give me my change back; you must take this to where you got it"—he gave me sixpence and fourpence-halfpenny, which I had given him in change, and 1 1/2 d. for the stout and., mild—there was a lot more money in his hand—I do not generally give counterfeit coin back—when I had given it back he wrapped it up in a piece of paper, and put it in his pocket—my son had observed him, and followed him.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I only tried the shilling with my teeth—I recognise it.
JOHN STEVENS . I am the son of the last witness—I was present when the prisoner was there—I saw the shilling tendered in the way my father has said, and I saw it wrapped up and the prisoner leave the house—I followed him to the Montpelier, in the Queen's Road, Peckham, half a mile off; he went very slowly; he had something in his hand which he was rubbing, and he rubbed it against corners—I could not see what it was—when I got to the Montpelier, after him, I saw the barmaid, Miss
Bardrick, trying the shilling between her teeth, and I said "You had better detain this man, as he tried to pass one at our place"—the prisoner said "I did not know it was bad, I thought it was a good one," and he said he thought my father had made a mistake and that it was a good one—Miss Bardrick kept the shilling—the prisoner had called for half a pint of stout and mild—he afterwards gave sixpence in payment, and she gave him 4 1/2 d. in change—he went out, I followed him, saw a sergeant of police, and spoke to him, and the prisoner was given in charge.
ROSINA BARDRICK . I am barmaid at the Montpelier Tavern, Queen's Road, Peckham—on the evening of 2nd November the prisoner came in and tendered a shilling in payment for some stout and mild—I was trying it in my teeth when young Mr. Stevens came in—he said "You had better detain the man, as he tried the same game at my father's house"—the prisoner then gave me sixpence, and 1 have him 4 1/2 d. in change—I kept the shilling and afterwards handed it to the constable.
THOMAS DURHAM ( Sergeant P 5). On the afternoon of 2nd Nov. the younger Stevens spoke to me and pointed out the prisoner—I went to the Montpelier, where I received this shilling—I took the prisoner and said "I shall take you into custody for uttering a counterfeit coin at the Alliance public-house and also at the Montpelier public-house, Queen's Road, Peckham"—he said "I know nothing of it"—I took him to the station, searched him, and found on him a pocket-book containing 257 penny stamps in sheets of 36, 18, 15, 12, 8, 6, 4, 3, and 2; half a sovereign in gold, 18 s. in silver, and 6 ¼ d. in bonze, all good—when I searched him he said "I do not know where I got it from," pointing to the shilling, "I think it was at Rochester, where I sold some clothes. I tried to pass it the second time because I thought it was good. The stamps I had sent me by friends "—I said" Where do your friends live?"—he said "I don't want them to know anything about it, I shall not tell you"—with regard to his address he said "I don't want my friends to know; I shall refuse to give you my address."
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. The were 257 stamps in the pocket-book. WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER. This shilling is bad.
Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "I must have taken the shilling in the country. I must have gone to the second house thinking they were under a mistake about it."
The prisoner, in his defence, stated that after the first uttering he had compared the shilling with other money and thought it was perfectly good, and so took it to the second public-house; and contended that it was not likely a man with 50 s. in his pocket would go about with a bad shilling.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant
[trialAccount t18821120-98]
98. EDWARD GILROY (38) , Unlawfully uttering a counterfeit florin to Ann Newman at Dorking.
MR. BAGOALLAY Prosecuted; MR. THORNE COLE Defended. WILLIAM WALTON. I am landlord of the Three Tuns public-house, Dorking—I know the prisoner Gilroy, and I also know Seabrook ( See next case —I remember their coming to my house on the evening of 8th September—as far; is I can recollect it was about half-past 9 o'clock, I won't be quite sure as to the time—Gilroy spoke; he said, "I want accommodation
for myself and friend "—I asked him his name—he said," Gilroy; we want accommodation! we want a private room, but one room will do for us both"—I said, "I have only one room left, and you may have that; What are you and what is your name!"—he said, "We are clerks on our holiday down from London"—I said, "What name?"—he said, "Mr. Hollis and friend "— Gilroy then asked whether the town was large—they had something to drink, nut they hardly drank anything—they then went out, and Gilroy came back about 10.30, but I did not see Seabrook again till now—when Gilroy came back he remained till he was taken in custody.
Cross-examined. It was Gilroy who took the bed—I thought at first that we were quite full, but I referred to my book and found that we had one room vacant—he did not say that he would rather pay something more and have a bed to himself—he did not give me eighteenpence—I got paid; Gilroy paid me 2 s. in good money—he came back about 9.30, and about 10.30 he went to bed and occupied the bed which he had paid the 2 s. for, and he went to that bed alone; no one else was in the same room—it was about 20 minutes after that that the police came, that would be about 20 minutes to 11—Dorking is about 23 miles from London—I can't remember at what time the last train leaves there for London—I have lived there about a year and eight months—there is a police inspector here.
HARRIET BOOKING . I live at Dorking, and am the landlady of the Bell Inn—I remember two men coming there on the night of the 8th September, about 9.30, it might have been a little before, but it was not after 9.30—I identified the prisoner and Seabrook as the men—Seabrook asked for two cups of bitter ale; I served them with one each—Seabrook paid me with a florin, and I gave him 1 s. 8 d. change—I put the florin into the till; this is it ( produced)—I marked it in the presence of the superintendent before I gave it to him—I pat it in the till, and shortly afterwards my attention was called to it by Superintendent Lambert—I then took it out of the till; it was the only one there—since the prisoners bad left I had taken no other florin—it was the only one I had in that till.
Cross-examined. The Bell is a commercial inn, and we have commercial men constantly coming in and out—we do a very fair business—we have two tills; I cannot say how much was in the two, but I had very little silver in that till; we never keep money in it—my husband counted it; there might be a fourpenny or threepenny-piece—I did not count it; I had but a few shillings—my husband is not here—I saw him count it; there was not more than five or six shillings; there was not ten shillings there.
MARY ANN BUTLER . I live at the Bull's Head, Dorking, and am the daughter of the landlady—I was there on the evening of 8th Sept., and remember two men coming in, the prisoner is one of them—it was 10 o'clock, or it might have been a few minutes before, as near as I can judge—I served him with threepenny worth of cold brandy—he tendered a florin in payment, and then went out of the house—I remember Superintendent Lambert coming; that might be 10 minutes afterwards, because it is a very little distance from the police-station—I had sent for him when I discovered the second coin—I took that from the prisoner Sea-brook, who came in immediately after Gilroy had left, and tendered me a florin—I returned it to him, as I found it was bad, and asked him to
give me good money, and he gave me a shilling, which was good—I then discovered in Seabrook's presence that the florin which Gilroy had given me was bad, and thereupon sent for Superintendent Lambert, and handed him the coin which Gilroy had given me, after marking it.
Cross-examined. It is the fact that I discovered that I had a counterfeit coin before the police or anybody came in and spoke to me—it was then a few minutes to 10 o'clock as near as possible, and I sent my pot-man out for a policeman, and told him to follow—he did not come back for some time, half-past 10 or a quarter to 11 o'clock—I did not see Gilroy again till 11 o'clock, when he was brought to our house for me to recognise as he passed the station—Stingimore the police constable brought him to the front door, not into the house—I was asked to go out, and he said "Is this the man?"—Stingimore is a Dorking police-man—my potman is not here.
ANN NEWMAN . I keep the tap at the Bed Lion Hotel, Dorking—on the night of 8th Sept. two men came in late, from a quarter to 20 minutes past 10 o'clock—I identify the prisoner and Seabrook as the men—Gilroy asked for a pint of ale—I served them, and Gilroy gave me a two-shilling piece to pay for it—I gave him 1 s. 9 d. change, in good money—they were there about six minutes, and left together—Superintendent Lambert came in five or six minutes afterwards, and I showed him the two-shilling piece which Gilroy had given me—I then marked it and laid it on a shelf close by, and next morning I gave it to Superintendent Lambert—I examined the till when Lambert came in, and that was the only two-shilling piece in the till at that time.
Cross-examined. When the two-shilling piece was taken out of the till there were only a few shillings and a few sixpences there, which had been taken that evening—I was at work in the bar all the evening and that was the money taken from the customers.
GEORGE LAMBERT . I am superintendent of the Dorking Police—on the night of September 8th, about 10 o'clock, I received information with regard to some counterfeit coin—I went in search of the prisoners, and found Seabrook in the High Street—I took him to the Bull's Head, and afterwards to the police-station—I searched him at the Bull's Head and found on him this counterfeit florin ( produced) and 2 l. 18 s. in good money—there were two sovereigns, one half-crown, five shillings, twelve six-pences, three threepenny pieces, and 8 s. 9 d. in copper—I afterwards saw Gilroy at the police-station about 11 o'clock.
Cross-examined. I was present when Gilroy was searched—Stingimore searched him, and he has got the money with the exception of the gold—he can give you exact evidence as to the money found—I only found one counterfeit coin on Seabrook—the last train leaves Dorking for London on the South-Eastern line about half-past 9, and on the London, Brighton, and South Coast about 5 minutes to 10 at night—I know the potman at the Bull's Head—I know that the landlady and the mother sent the potman out on this evening, but do not know at what time—I did not see him come out—I saw the potman in the High Street between 10 o'clock and the time that Gilroy was taken in custody—I did not see him in a public-house treating Gilroy to something to drink—I do not know that the potman was in Gilroy's company at all—I do not know that he gave him something to drink, and that he went from there home to the bed which he had engaged.
JOHN STINGIMORE . I am a police-constable of Dorking—on the night of the 8th of September I was sent to the Three Tuns Inn about 11 o'clock, and saw the prisoner Gilroy there in bed—I said "I want you on a charge of uttering counterfeit coin"—he said "I know nothing about it"—I asked him to get up—he put on his things and went with me to the station—I searched him both there and at the station, and found 3 l. 14 s. 8 d.—there was 1 sovereign, 2 half sovereigns, 2 florins, 13 shillings, 36 sixpences, 1 threepenny piece, and 4 s. 5 d. in bronze, all good money—the public-houses close at 11 o'clock.
Cross-examined. I took Gilroy to Mrs. Butler at the Bull's Head to be identified—I asked her if she knew him—there are about 9,000 people in Dorking and there is one superintendent and four policemen to look after the 9,000—these depositions were taken at the Dorking Police-court.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . I am one of the inspectors of coin to Her Majesty's Hint—these coins are all bad and from the same mould.
By the JURY. I arrive at that conclusion because these counterfeit pieces are cast in a plaster-of-Paris mould from a good coin, which is termed the pattern piece, and all its imperfections are reproduced in the copies—they are of course all of the same date—I am not bound to point out in open Court where the imperfections are, but I can see them with my naked eye.
Cross-examined. You will find the date on the obverse side.
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18821120-99]
99. EDWARD GILROY (38) was again indicted, with HENRY SEABROOK (23) , for unlawfully uttering a counterfeit florin to Mary Ann Butler, another to Ann Newman, and another to Harriet Booking. MR. BAGGALLAY Prosecuted; MR. THORNS COLE, who defended Gilroy, withdrew from the case in consequence of the former verdict. WILLIAM WALTON . The evidence given by this witness in the last case was read over to. him from the short-hand writer's notes, to which he assented.
Cross-examined by Seabrook. I can swear that you were with your fellow-prisoner.
HARRIET BOOKING . The evidence of this witness was read over to her, to which she assented.
Cross-examined by Seabrook. It was nearly half past nine when you called at our house but not not quite—I did not find that the coin was bad till Superintendent Lambert came in, and asked if I had taken any bad coin—that was at 10 o'clock or shortly after—no one was with you but Gilroy.
MARY ANN BUTLER , The evidence of this witness was read over to her, to which she assented, and added: I gave Gilroy If. 9 d. change—when I handed Seabrook the florin I said, "It is bad money, give me a good one "—I didnot notice that he made any reply, but he handed me a good shilling end I gave him tenpence change.
By the COURT. I know the coin was bad because I felt it very greasy in my fingers and I did not put it in the till, till I had said to a person in the bar "This is a bad one, is it not?" and he said "A very bad one" in the prisoner's presence—I did not ring it, but he threw it on the counter himself—it did not sound much because it is a pewter counter.
Cross-examined by Seabrook. When I was asked if I could recognise you I did not Say that I could not swear to you.
ANN NEWMAN . The evidence of this witness was read over to her, to which she assented.
Cross-examined by Seabrook. I did not find out that the coin was bad till I was told so by Superintendent Lambert about 25 minutes or half past ten—you were in company with Gilroy when you called at oar house.
GEORGE LAMBERT . The evidence of this witness was read over, to which is assented.
Cross-examined by Seabrook. When I arrested you, I took you to the landlady where you passed the coin—I asked her if you were one of the men, and she said "Yes."
JOHN STINGIMORE and WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER repeated their former evidence.
Seabrook's Defence. I arrived at Dorking by myself—I do not know the prisoner charged with me—I know nothing about passing these coins—the last coin found on me was returned by the lady, and I wrapped it up in paper—if I had known it was bad I should hare thrown it away.
GUILTY . GILROY was further charged ( on the first Indictment) with a previous conviction at this Court in the name of Thomas Parsons of feloniously uttering counterfeit coin in November, 1875; to this he PLEADED GUILTY. GILROY **.— Six Years' Penal Servitude. SEABROOK.— Fifteen Months Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18821120-100]
100. ANNIE BURNS (40) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND and MATHEWS Prosecuted. JAKE SUTTON. I am assistant to Mr. Clarke at the refreshment bar, Waterloo Station—on 29th August I served the prisoner with a Bath bun, price twopence—she gave me a half-crown—I put it in the tester, bent it, and gave it back to her, and she gave me a good florin and left—on the next day she came again to another bar where I was serving, and was served with a glass of ale—she tendered this shilling—I put it in the tester, and found it was bad—I gave it to Mr. Hopgood.
MARGARET GREGG . I am a barmaid at Waterloo Station—on 12th September the prisoner came and asked for a glass of milk, which cams to twopence—she paid with a florin, and 1 s. 10 d. change was given her—the florin was put in the till—there were no other florins there—I afterwards examined it, found it was bad, and gave it to Miss Seal, who gave it back to the prisoner, and the prisoner gave back the change and paid for what she had—I have no doubt she is the person.
ELIZABETH SEAL . I am a barmaid at Waterloo Station—on 12th September the prisoner, I believe, but I won't swear to her, had some milk, and paid me with a florin—I put it in the till—there were no other florins there, only small silver—Miss Gregg afterwards took it out and handed it to the prisoner.
CHARLES HOPGOOD . I am manager to Mr. Clarke at Waterloo Station—on 10th October this shilling was given to me—I fetched a constable, who spoke to the prisoner—I did not near what she said—I gave him the shilling and said to her" You have been trying to pass bad money, and I am afraid this is not the first time"—she said,' 'I know nothing about it." RICHARD EDMUNDS. I am a detective employed by the London and South-Western Railway—Mr. Hopgood took me to the prisoner and showed me this shilling—I told her I should detain her till a metropolitan?
constable was fetched, and she would be charged with uttering a bad shilling—she made no reply—I handed her over to Warren with the shilling.
WILLIAM WARREN ( Policeman). I took the prisoner and received this shilling—she said that she was not aware it was bad—a gentleman gave it to her the previous night with 10 s.—on the way to the station she took a good florin from her pocket—a good half-crown and shilling were found in her purse—Mrs. Sutton said in the prisoner's hearing that she was there some days before and tendered bad money, the prisoner made no reply.
MR. JOHN WEBSTER , This shilling is bad.
The Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "I know nothing about it; I received it from a gentleman the night before with some other silver."
GUILTY of uttering the shilling only.— Four Months' Hard Labour.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, DECEMBER 1 ST, 1882.
